# LORD
## EXETER.

->MY LORD,<-

You have always shown such zeal for the concerns of your friends that I cannot doubt you will pardon the liberty I take on behalf of one of the best of men. This favor is to beseech you to be so kind to him as to take the trouble <note>original: "take the pains"</note> to read over his book. He was about to submit <note>original: "engage"</note> it for the judgment of [someone], which my friend, the author, promised to publish; since I know some authors are apt to think as highly of what they <unclear>write</unclear> as they possibly <unclear>can</unclear>.

### MY LORD,

**YOU** have always shown yourself so generous to your friends that I cannot help but believe you will pardon the liberty I take on behalf of one of the best of mine. I told him plainly, *that you consider it a great point of honor never to say anything but what you think, and that he should not flatter himself by imagining you would set aside a quality so rare, and so new at Court, as to say his book is good if you find it otherwise.* But what I now desire of your Lordship, and what I humbly beg you will not deny me, is to decide a disagreement <note>original: "Difference"</note> we have had between us. You should not, my Lord, have accumulated for yourself such a prodigious store of knowledge and learning if you did not intend to be consulted before even the greatest scholars <note>original: "Doctors"</note>. *This, Sir, is the dispute which I have had with my friend.*

I tried to persuade him to change the whole framework of his work; for this joking <note>original: "Drolling," meaning a mocking or satirical tone</note> manner of carrying it out does not seem appropriate to his subject to me. These mysteries of the Kabbalah <note>original: "Cabal," referring here to mystical or secret occult traditions</note> (*I said to him*) are serious matters which many of my friends study seriously. # A Letter, &c.

we have—enough to make a *wise man* laugh indeed at *any subject whatsoever*—are certainly most dangerous to joke with. *And we may very well fear that it might bring a scandal upon our religion.* It is true, he must make a *Kabbalist* <note>original: "Cabalist," a practitioner of the Kabbalah, a form of Jewish mysticism that was often conflated with occultism in this period</note> speak like a *saint*, or else he improperly manages the *character*; and if he does speak like a *saint*, he deceives those of weak *judgment* by this appearance of *sanctity*. Probably, he shall induce *men* to believe in these *wild fantasies* <note>original: "Extravagancies"</note> more than all his *joking* <note>original: "Drolling," meaning a mocking or satirical tone</note> shall be able to *refute*.

To this, *my friend* answered with the same *presumption* that *authors* are accustomed to show when they defend their *works*: that though these *Kabbalistic mysteries* are so serious, there are none but *melancholy people* <note>original: "Melancholly Persons"; in the 17th century, melancholy was believed to be a physical imbalance that led to obsessive or irrational behavior</note> who devote themselves to the *study* of them. He argued that should he attack this *subject* directly in a *formal, academic method* <note>original: "Dogmatick Method"</note>, he himself would become *ridiculous* for treating such *fooleries* seriously. *And he judged it more to the purpose to turn all this ridicule on the Count of Gabalis.* <note>The Count of Gabalis is the title character of a famous 1670 satirical novel by Henri de Montfaucon de Villars, which mocked the occult sciences.</note> The *secret sciences* of the *Kabbalah* <note>original: "Cabal"</note> are among those *illusions* <note>original: "Chymæras," referring to the mythical Chimera; here meaning impossible or foolish fantasies</note>—

# A Letter, &c.

[Chime]ras <note>original: "mæras"; the end of the word "Chymæras," meaning illusions or wild fantasies</note>; to which we give the most authority when we argue against them seriously. He argued that we should not, therefore, attempt to defeat and destroy them except by mockery. Since he was well-read in the Church Fathers <note>Early Christian theologians whose writings established orthodox doctrine</note>, he cited **Tertullian** <note>An influential early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155–220 AD)</note> to me. Your **Lordship**, who understands these writers better than either **he** or **I** do, may judge whether he has quoted him accurately: "*Many things deserve to be defeated by laughter, so that they are not given undue honor through seriousness.*" <note>original Latin: "Multa sunt risu digna revinci, ne gravitate adorentur." From Tertullian’s work against the Valentinians.</note> He said that **Tertullian** used this excellent saying against the **Valentinians** <note>A major Gnostic movement in the early Christian church</note>, who were a type of fanatical **Rosicrucians** <note>A secret spiritual and cultural movement that claimed to possess esoteric wisdom from the ancient past</note> in his time.

Regarding the devotion that appears in almost every part of this work: he said it is an unavoidable necessity that a **Kabbalist** <note>original: "Cabalist," a practitioner of the Kabbalah, a form of Jewish mysticism</note> should speak of God. But then again, it is noticeable in this book that there is an even greater necessity for maintaining this **Kabbalistic character**—namely, that God is never spoken of except with extreme respect. Consequently, religion cannot be harmed by it. However, those with weak judgment will be even more infatuated with it than the **Lord Gabalis** <note>The fictional Count of Gabalis, the title character of the book being satirized</note> himself, if they allow themselves to be bewitched by this extravagant devotion;

<summary>The author argues that mystical fantasies are best refuted through satire rather than serious debate, citing the Church Father Tertullian as a precedent. He notes that while the book speaks of God with respect to maintain its "Kabbalistic character," this piety might actually trick gullible readers into believing its absurdities.</summary>

# A Letter, etc.

...if the raillery used here does not obstruct the charm.

By these his reasons, (my Lord) and many others which I shall refrain from repeating to you (because I am very ambitious to bring you over to my side), my friend claims he ought to have written against the Kabbalah <note>original: "Cabal"; here referring to a secret tradition of mystical interpretation</note> in this pleasant manner. Your Lordship, I hope, will be pleased to decide this controversy. I affirm that it would be most proper to proceed against the Kabbalists <note>original: "Cabalists"</note>, or <term>Rosicrucians</term> <note>original: "Rosy-Crufians"; a secret society of mystics claiming to possess esoteric wisdom</note>, and against all their secret sciences, with serious and vigorous arguments. He says that Truth is naturally brisk and gay, and that she is most powerful when she laughs. He says this because one of the ancients <note>original: "Antients"</note>—whom without doubt you know, and whose words you cannot help but call to mind, being endowed with such a faithful memory—says in a certain place: "*It is fitting for truth to laugh, because it is joyful.*" <note>original Latin: "Convenit veritati ridere, quia lætans." This is another reference to Tertullian.</note>

He added that the secret sciences are dangerous if we do not deal with them in such a way that inspires us with contempt for them—specifically by depicting their ridiculous mysteries and preventing the world from losing time

# The Count

trusted his treasures to me deserved <note>original: "merited"</note> any punishment. You shall see how everything happened.

Common sense having always made me suspect that there was a great deal of emptiness in everything they call *secret sciences* <term>secret sciences: the occult or "hermetic" arts, such as alchemy, astrology, and magic, which were kept hidden from the uninitiated</term>, I was never tempted to waste so much time as to turn the pages <note>original: "Leaves"</note> of the books which discuss them. Yet, not finding it reasonable to condemn—without knowing why—all those who devote themselves to them (who are otherwise wise people, mostly very learned, and eminent in both the *gown* and the *sword* <term>gown and sword: "the gown" refers to professions of law, medicine, or divinity, while "the sword" refers to military service and the nobility</term>), I made a resolution. In order to avoid being unjust or wearing myself out with tedious reading, I decided to pretend <note>original: "feigning"</note> to be a great devotee of these sciences among all those whom I could learn were

# of Gabalis.

of that group <note>original: "Gang"</note>. I quickly achieved more success than I could have possibly hoped for. Because all these gentlemen, however mysterious and reserved they may seem to be, desire nothing more than to give voice to their imaginations and the "new discoveries" which they pretend to have made in nature. Within a few days, I was the confidant of the most prominent <note>original: "Considerable"</note> among them, and every day I had one or another of them in my study, which I had purposefully decorated <note>original: "garnish'd"</note> with their most fantastical authors. There was never a learned *virtuoso* <term>virtuoso: an intellectual or person with a specialized interest in the arts or sciences; here, an expert in esoteric or occult knowledge</term> of this kind with whom I did not have some correspondence. In short, because of my zeal for this "science," <note>original: "Science" — here referring to the study of the occult, alchemy, and the hermetic arts</note> I quickly found that I was well-regarded by all. I had as my companions princes, great lords, men of the law <term>gown-men: members of professional classes, such as lawyers, judges, or clergy, who wore professional robes</term>, handsome ladies (and plain ones too), doctors, and prelates,

<header>The Count</header>

...prelates, friars, and nuns: in short <note>original: "In fine"</note>, people of all ranks and social standings. Some of them were interested in communicating <note>original: "Converse"</note> with *angels*, others with *devils*, others with their *guardian spirits* <note>original: "Genius"</note>, and others with *incubi* <note>original: "Incubus's"</note>. Some devoted themselves to curing diseases, some to astronomy <note>original: "Star-gazing"</note>, some to the secrets of theology <note>original: "Divinity"</note>, and almost all to the *philosopher's stone*.

They all agreed that these "grand secrets"—and especially the *philosopher's stone*—were very difficult to discover, and that only a few people ever attain them. But each of them had such a high opinion of himself as to believe that he was among the *elect* <term>elect: in this context, those chosen by God or nature to receive hidden, divine knowledge</term>. Fortunately, the most prominent among them were at this time waiting with infinite impatience for the arrival of a lord who was a great *cabalist* <term>cabalist: a student of the Kabbalah, a mystical tradition; in the 17th century, the term often referred more broadly to someone pursuing secret or magical knowledge</term>, and whose estate lies upon

## Discourse the Second

The Count was pleased to allow me the entire night for prayer. In the morning, at daybreak, he informed me by a note that he would come to my house by eight o'clock and that, if I liked, we might go out and take the air together. I waited for him; he arrived, and after we had exchanged pleasantries <note>original: "Reciprocal Civilities"</note>, he said to me, "Let us go to some place where we may be free together, and where nobody may interrupt our conversation."

"*Rueil* <note>original: "Ruel"; likely referring to Rueil-Malmaison, a town near Paris known for its pleasant atmosphere and the estate of Cardinal Richelieu</note>," I said to him, "seems to be a very pleasant and solitary place." 

"Let us go there then," he replied.

# The Count

He inspired them with the realization that just as man—through the alliance he formed with *God*—has been made a sharer in divinity, so too the *sylphs*, *gnomes*, *nymphs*, and *salamanders* might be made sharers in immortality through the alliance they might form with man.

# The Count

For about a month, one will see the flying republic <note>original: "volant Re-publique," meaning a flying society or community</note> of the *Sylphs* in the air; the *Nymphs* come in shoals up the rivers, and the guardians of treasures <note>referring to the Gnomes</note> present you with their riches. They require no worship from the *Sages*, since they know well enough that a human is nobler than they are. Thus, venerable Nature teaches her children how to restore <note>original: "repair"</note> the elements by using the elements themselves. Thus, harmony is re-established. Thus, man recovers his natural empire and can do all things within the elements without the help of a *demon* <note>original: "Dæmon," here referring to meddling spirits or devils as opposed to natural elemental spirits</note> or any unlawful art. Thus you see, my son, that the *Sages* are more innocent than you thought. You have nothing to say to me—"

Neither, my child," he continued, smiling, "is it for you to break the marriage that a great *cardinal* has made between *David* and the *Sibyl*; <note>A reference to the famous liturgical line from the *Dies Irae*: "Teste David cum Sibylla" ("As David and the Sibyl testify"), which presents the pagan prophetess and the biblical King as joint witnesses to the coming Judgment.</note> nor should you accuse this knowledgeable person for having placed a great *prophet* and a miserable *energumen* <note>original: "Energumena"; a person possessed by a spirit, often used to refer to someone possessed by a demon.</note> in the same parallel. ## An ORACLE.

There is above the *Celestial Fire* <note>original: "Celestial Fire"; in Neoplatonic thought, this refers to the highest, most spiritual form of energy or the realm of the stars.</note>, an *Incorruptible Flame* that is always *sparkling*; the *Spring of Life*, the *Fountain of all Beings*, the *Origin of all Things!* This *Flame produces all Things*; and *nothing perishes* except for what it *consumes*. He argues that the body's coarseness <note>original: *grofeneſs*</note> only clouds our contemplations <term>speculations</term> and does not contribute to producing them at all. 

Blind *Aristotle*! Since you believe that our physical body cannot be the source of our spiritual thoughts, what do you mean by suggesting that a weak vapor <term>exhalation</term> <note>original: *Exhalation*—Aristotle and other ancient philosophers often attributed the prophetic trances of oracles to physical gases or vapors rising from the earth.</note> can be the cause of such sublime thoughts? How can such a vapor cause the trances <term>raptures</term> that seized the *Pythians* <note>original: *Phythians*—the priestesses of Apollo at the Oracle of Delphi, who were said to be inspired by vapors from a chasm.</note> when they delivered their oracles? 

You can easily see, my child, that this great mind <term>wit</term> fell short, and that his desire to be unconventional <term>singularity</term> was the cause of it. 

"You reason very justly, Sir," I said to him, being...

"I would say to him," I answered, "My Lord *Danhuzerus*, with all due respect, you are a little mad, or else your vision is witchcraft. Your children and your mistress are filthy demons <note>original: "Fiends"</note>, and I am of the opinion that your conscience is completely hardened <note>original: "seared to some purpose"; a biblical reference to a conscience that has lost its moral sensitivity.</note>."

## THE
# TRANSLATOR’S
## Observations
### ON THE
## Foregoing Discourses.

I have ventured to translate this tract during my spare time, as I was much affected by the strange curiosity of the <term>Cabalistic sciences</term> <note>The Cabala (or Kabbalah) is a tradition of Jewish mysticism, though in this era, "Cabalistic" often referred more broadly to various forms of occultism or esoteric philosophy.</note>. This work somewhat resembles a <term>philosophical romance</term> <note>A fictional story used to illustrate philosophical points; the translator is suggesting the work is more imaginative than factual.</note> and is as mythical and weak as an <term>old monk’s legend</term>. 

In it, you will find the <term>Cabalist</term> to be a miserable, blind creature, fit for a guide dog and a bell <note>A reference to the common equipment used by blind beggars in the early modern period.</note>; yet in his own conceit <note>original: "conceit"; meaning vanity or self-importance</note>, he believes himself to have more vision than all the world and to be best qualified for the office of a guide. He is much devoted to idle traditions, and by that "crooked line" he measures both religion and reason. He is a great hater of women, yet much addicted to <term>sexual desire</term> <note>original: "Venery"</note> in a "philosophical" way. 

In a word, he is a creature of much choler— <note>The original text breaks off here at "Chola-", likely intended to be "choler," referring to the "choleric" temperament associated with irritability and anger in the theory of the four humors.</note>

<summary>In this preface, the translator introduces a work on Cabalism, dismissing the subject matter as a weak, mythical "romance" and criticizing the character of the Cabalist as a blind, vain, and contradictory figure.</summary>
<keywords>Cabalism, mysticism, translator's preface, philosophical romance, occultism, gender views</keywords>

# The Translators

...choler and little brains. His madness might make you laugh, but his folly will sometimes grieve you.

I presume that no one but a Frenchman would have first taken the trouble to collect and give such credit to these Cabalistic whimsies <note>original: "Whymfeys"; meaning fanciful, irrational, or superstitious ideas</note> as my author seems to do. That nation, being so airy and so amorous, may boast if it wishes that it is descended from the **Sylphs** <note>In the elemental system of the physician Paracelsus, Sylphs were the invisible spirits of the Air.</note>, those *brisk inhabitants of the air* mentioned in this preceding treatise. I shall not envy them if they say that **Pharamond** <note>A legendary first King of the Franks (ancestors of the French), often cited in historical myths of the time.</note>, the founder of their kingdom, was fathered by the salamander <note>In elemental philosophy, salamanders were the spirits of Fire.</note> **Oromasis**, or any other of his fiery successors. Yet, to the comfort of an Englishman, the Greek historians tell us that **Xerxes** <note>The Persian King who invaded Greece in 480 BCE. He famously ordered a canal cut through a peninsula (sailing through the "continent") and built a bridge of ships across the sea (walking on the "deep").</note>, who "sailed upon the continent and walked upon the deep"—and who was so imprudent as to whip **Neptune** <note>The Roman god of the sea (Poseidon in Greek). When a storm destroyed Xerxes' bridge across the Hellespont, he famously ordered the water to be given three hundred lashes as punishment.</note> and to bluster against the North Wind—was at last easily beaten...

# Observations

...beaten by the people who, by the order of Providence, trusted in their "wooden walls" <note>The "wooden walls" refer to the ships of a navy—a classic metaphor for British maritime power.</note>. 

But regarding this Cabalistic <note>Cabalistic refers to the study of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah), which in this period was often used to describe occult or esoteric theories about spirits and secret knowledge.</note> discourse that my Frenchman has collected for us, he pretends to mock it, but does not do so effectively. Instead, like a habitual liar who has told falsehoods for so long he no longer recognizes them, he seems unaware of his own errors. It is appropriate, therefore, to use classical authors to refute his empty hypothesis, even though he claims to use those same authors to defend his own arguments.

Pagan writers—both philosophers and poets—never mention more than two types of intellectual beings besides the gods: namely, the demi-gods and the souls of men. These demi-gods <note>original: "Semidei"</note> were always considered immortal by the pagans because of the will of Jupiter. Plato introduces Jupiter speaking to them in this way: "You indeed cannot be immortal by nature, but by my power you shall be immortal" <note>original Latin: "Vos quidem, Immortales esse non potestis, sed mea potestate Immortales eritis." This is a reference to a passage in Plato's *Timaeus* where the creator addresses the lesser gods.</note>. 

As for the souls of men, because they could not deny their immortality for solid reasons—yet were unable to understand the specific circumstances of our future state—they were...

But in all ages, speculative men—even in their most subtle metaphysics—could never be considered to be in their right minds if they presumed the existence of *half-humans* <note>original: *Semi-homines*</note> that inhabited the elements. These were creatures supposedly composed of "material" souls and bodies—inferior, yet superior to man in their faculties and powers. *What a strange contradiction! That beings consisting only of matter should act in a way that is impossible and against their nature; since matter—according to the axioms and established laws of all philosophers, whether Academics, Peripatetics, Stoics, or Cynics <note>These represent the major schools of ancient Greek philosophy: the followers of Plato (Academics), Aristotle (Peripatetics), Zeno (Stoics), and Diogenes (Cynics).</note>—is not able to truly act or think.* Now, to overthrow the assertion regarding these "elementary people" <note>In the context of this book, "elementary people" refers to elemental spirits—sylphs, undines, gnomes, and salamanders—which the author of the original French text claimed inhabited the four elements.</note>—

# Animadversions
<note>original: "Animadverſions". This term refers to critical remarks or a critique of a specific text or idea.</note>

...at once; and by undermining the foundation, to destroy the superstructure: Let us consider what can be more *absurd* or *fallacious* in **logic** and **philosophy** than to assume a *principle* for a *thing* that cannot exist, and then to attach a *conclusion* based on most *erroneous premises*. To propose a *creature* whose *essence* and *form* is *mortal*, and consequently *material*, yet at the same time to make this *creature* divine in its *faculties*, *sharp* in its *operations*, and *superior* to *man* in its *understanding*—whether regarding *divine* or *human objects*—is a contradiction. Such qualities can proceed from nothing but an *immortal* and *immaterial cause*. Let any thinking *person* judge whether such *notions* do not deserve **hellebore** <note>original: "Hellibore". A plant used in ancient medicine as a purge to treat "madness" or insanity.</note> to purge them, rather than *disputation* to confute them? And whether **Bedlam** <note>original: "Bedlam". The Bethlehem Royal Hospital in London, a notorious asylum for the mentally ill.</note> is not the fittest *academy* for those who *believe* in them?

This *Kabbalistic fancy* <note>original: "Cabaliſtick Fancy". Refers to the mystical and occult traditions the author is critiquing.</note>, certainly in its *origin*, seems exactly to mimic the *follies* of the *Quran* <note>original: "Alcoran". An archaic spelling of the Quran.</note>. The *poets* of old told a story of three gods urinating into an ox’s hide to create the famous **Orion** <note>This refers to a myth where Jupiter, Neptune, and Mercury created Orion from an ox-hide they had urinated upon.</note>; in the same way, the ingredients of **Arianism**, **Judaism**, and **paganism** <note>original: "Ethuiciſm". Likely a variant of "Ethnicism," a term used at the time to refer to paganism or the religions of the "heathen" nations.</note> composed a reli-

**Oromasis** <note>original: "Oromasis." A corruption of Ahura Mazda, the supreme deity in Zoroastrianism, whom the Kabbalists here treat as a prince of fire-dwellers.</note>, according to their wild fantasies <note>original: "Chymæras." Chimeras; in this context, refers to absurd or impossible ideas.</note>, being Prince of the People who inhabit the fire, had two children by **Vesta**, the wife of **Noah**: namely, **Zoroaster** and **Egeria**. **Zoroaster**, a great hero and the inventor of magic, is reported by **Justin** <note>Justin (Marcus Junianus Justinus) was a Roman historian of the 2nd century AD.</note> to have been King of the **Bactrians** <note>An ancient Iranian people of Central Asia.</note>; yet he was overcome by **Ninus** <note>The legendary founder of Nineveh and King of Assyria.</note>, despite all his magic and valor, living (according to this calculation) around the end of Noah's life. 

Now, how do the Kabbalists <note>original: "Cabalists."</note> know that Noah's wife (supposing her name was Vesta) was not already dead before her son Zoroaster was born? And it is very probable that Justin is mistaken, calling him "Zoroaster" when **Ctesias** <note>A 5th-century BC Greek physician and historian.</note> calls the same King of the Bactrians "**Oxiartes**." For **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the first-century "Natural History."</note> doubts very much whether magic has been around for so long; and if **Xanthus the Lydian** <note>A 5th-century BC historian of Lydia.</note>, a very ancient writer, reckons only 600 years between Zoroaster and **Xerxes** <note>The Persian King who invaded Greece in 480 BC.</note>, then Zoroaster must have lived a hundred years after the **Trojan Wars**. Regarding the oracles, where my author seems most pleased with himself: It is very easy to prove who inspired them, but I will refute the Kabbalist using Porphyry <note>Porphyry of Tyre (c. 234–305 AD), a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on oracles and spirits.</note>, because the author values him so highly. In the preceding book, he establishes as a rule that the Devil is not at liberty to roam at his own pleasure, nor could it be he who spoke through the oracles; for the Devil would never say anything against his own interests, which he would have to do if he were the one pronouncing them. Therefore, he argues that the oracles must have been spoken by some member of the **elementary people** <note>In occult philosophy, these are spirits belonging to the four elements—earth, air, fire, and water—such as gnomes or sylphs.</note> who, knowing God, spoke of Him with such reverence. To this, I propose only one oracle from Porphyry; if the author will admit that any of the elementary people spoke it, I am satisfied, and I must conclude that (if he can even prove they exist) they are worse than devils.

Porphyry, in his book *On the Philosophy of Oracles* <note>original Greek: Περὶ Θεολογίαν Φιλοσοφίας (*Peri Theologian Philosophias*). A work where Porphyry collected and commented on divine responses from oracles.</note>, says that a man inquired of Apollo which deity he should sacrifice to in order to turn his wife away from Christianity. The spirit <note>original: "Dæmon." In the ancient world, this referred to a supernatural being or lesser deity, not necessarily an evil one.</note> answered in Greek verse, to this effect:

> You would have better luck writing upon water, or stretching out your wings to fly like a bird through the air, than you would recovering the sanity of your wicked wife once she has been polluted in that manner. Let her go where she pleases, persisting in her empty delusions and singing hymns with useless lamentations to her "Crucified God," whom upright judges condemned and a shameful <note>original: "Ignominious"</note> death destroyed.

*I will not pretend, in this place, to deal with this cursed Porphyry <note>original: "Porphyrius." A Neoplatonist philosopher who was often critical of Christianity.</note>, since Saint Augustine has already done so quite thoroughly for me in this matter.* **The City of God, Book 19, Chapter 23.** <note>original Latin: "De Civitate Dei, Lib. 19, Cap. 23." Augustine's seminal work of Christian philosophy.</note>

Now, if the Devil spoke here, the <term>Kabbalist</term> <note>original: "Cabalist." Here referring to the author of the occult text being critiqued, who interprets spiritual mysteries through a specific mystical lens.</note> is proven wrong. If a <term>Sylph</term> <note>A mythological spirit of the air; one of the "elementary people" the author is debating.</note> uttered this, he is just as confounded <note>original: "Gravelled." To be stuck, perplexed, or run aground in an argument.</note>. Therefore, according to his own shaky <note>original: "precarious"</note> assumption, let him escape this dilemma if he can.

I will keep the reader no longer; I only ask that as he reads this, or any work of this kind, he might both laugh and marvel at the extravagant boldness of 
man's

<meta>catchword: Mans</meta>

<summary>The author uses a historical oracle recorded by Porphyry—which mocks Christ—to create a logical trap for his opponent's theory of nature spirits. He concludes by referencing Saint Augustine’s earlier refutation of these ideas.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Saint Augustine, City of God, Kabbalist, Sylph, Oracles, Christian Apologetics, Elementary Spirits</keywords>

# THE END. <note>original: "FINIS."</note>

->*ERRORS AND CORRECTIONS*<-
<note>original: "ERRATA." This is a list of corrections for mistakes found by the author or printer after the book was already printed.</note>

**P**age 6, line 23: should read *Devoto* <note>The Italian word for "devoted" or "devout."</note>. Page 8, line 24: should read *German Lord*. Page 10, line 20: should read *in of a most*. Page 40, last line <note>original: "l. ult.," an abbreviation for the Latin *linea ultima*.</note>: should read *Theophrastus Bombast* <note>The first two names of the physician and alchemist commonly known as Paracelsus (Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim).</note>. Page 43, line 4: for *which* should read *whom*. Page 42, line 8: for *of this Judgment* should read *in this Point*. Page 85, line 5: should read *weak exhalation* <note>original: "unpowerful exhalation." In early science, an "exhalation" referred to vapors or fumes thought to be released from the earth or atmosphere.</note>. In the same place <note>original: "ibid.," an abbreviation of the Latin *ibidem*.</note>, line 19: should read *Man*. Page 88, line 3: should read *his Folly*. Page 92, line 17: *Spirits* <note>original: "Daimonas," a Greek term for supernatural beings or divine powers; in this period, it could refer to both good and evil spirits.</note>. Page 95, line 16: for *seen* should read *heard of*. Page 96: should read *Rodrigo*. Page 114: should read *the twenty-first Chapter*. Page 115, line 22: for *Loins* <note>original: "Loyns."</note> should read *Loves*. Page 116: should read *Numa Pompilius* <note>The legendary second king of Rome, known for his wisdom and for establishing many of Rome's religious laws.</note>.
